#### (b) Cannot be checked from here — what to look at, and the right answer

**W-0 · ⚠ REVOKE THE GITHUB PAT PASTED INTO THE SESSION ON 2026-10-26. Delete this item once done.**
*What:* a fine-grained token beginning `github_pat_11AZ3S5…` was pasted into a Claude Code session as
plaintext, to authenticate `gh`. Revoke it at **github.com → Settings → Developer settings → Personal
access tokens → Fine-grained tokens**, and issue a replacement only by a route that does not cross a
transcript. *Why no agent:* only the token's owner can revoke it. *Why it must be revoked rather than
kept:* the session transcript is written to disk
(`~/.claude/projects/…/<session>.jsonl`, confirmed to contain it), and `/export` writes the whole
transcript into the working tree — it did exactly that once this session, as `apps/api/report.md`. That
file was deleted and `report.md` is now git-ignored, and the token never reached a commit (`gitleaks
detect` over all 83 commits: no leaks), but a secret that has been written to a file on disk is spent.
*How verified:* the token is absent from the fine-grained token list, and any `gh` call using it answers
`HTTP 401`. *The safe replacement route:* install `gh`, then run `gh auth login` **interactively** —
typing `! gh auth login` at the prompt — so the token is entered into `gh`'s own prompt and never appears
as command text. Never `--with-token` from a pasted string.

**W-1 · Paste the repo copy over the claude.ai Project instructions.**
*What:* replace the Project-instructions copy of `PROJECT_INSTRUCTIONS.md` **wholesale** with the current
repo file. *Why no agent:* the Project instructions live in the claude.ai Project, not the filesystem — no
session can read or write them, which is also why the drift was invisible. *How verified:* search the
pasted copy for four phrases — `Relay rule`, `Round limit`, `repo guard in CI`, `or in an error response
body`. Four searches, four hits.

**Wholesale, not paragraph by paragraph, and the count is the argument.** The repo copy has gained
**27 paragraphs across 10 sections** since Task 1 started (`task-0..HEAD`), not the two `§0` used to name:

| Section | Paragraphs gained | What they are |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `§0` Precedence | 2 | `BLUEPRINT.md` is an **archive**, not the original, and its §8 maps what is still binding |
| `§1` Locked stack | 5 | object storage, transactional mail, monorepo, tests, IaC rows (ADR-002, ADR-003) |
| `§3` Contradictions | 1 | C6's 300K design ceiling, launch tier set by Task 14 |
| `§4` Tenancy | 2 | two authentication surfaces; what guards Tasks 2-4 before Task 5 exists |
| `§4` Postgres traps | 4 | the worker expiry UPDATE's visibility rule · a bulk UPDATE/DELETE referencing no column **escapes RLS** · the repo guard in CI · data migrations under FORCE RLS |
| `§4` Realtime | 1 | **Redis carries no RLS** — every tenant key namespaced by tenant id |
| `§4` Edge & ops | 3 | load shedding implemented not stated · a declared fail-open/closed posture per dependency · no secrets in logs **or response bodies** |
| `§6` Universal gate | 4 | fresh clone not just a fresh DB · coverage ≥ 90% · shared contracts generated and CI-checked · OpenAPI committed and `/v1` versioned |
| `§11` Agent workflow | 3 | Relay rule · Round limit · the invariant named in the task's `§3` gate |
| `§12` Frontend bar | 2 | where the bar applies · direction approved in Task 8 before any component |

Reconciling ten sections by hand is how a copy drifts in the first place. One paste ends it.

**W-2 · GitHub: allow squash merging only. — ✅ DONE 2026-10-27.**
*Outcome:* the setting is applied, and it is verified by **W-2's own clause** — the merge button on PR #3
read **Squash and merge** with **no dropdown** offering the other two (observed by the user). Corroborated
in the repo rather than taken on the button alone: PR #3 landed as **`2e42267` with one parent**, so that
merge was in fact a squash. *Why it mattered:* R-001's "squash merge only" was the one clause in this
project broken **twice by hand** — `task-1`'s merge was a merge commit, and PR #2, created specifically to
be the first clean exercise of the rule after it failed, broke it again the same day. **This was never a
ruleset**, so R-001's "rulesets are not enforced on a private repository under an individual plan" never
applied to it: the merge-method checkboxes are plain repository settings and they bind on every plan. The
clause is no longer something anyone has to remember at the moment of merging — it is **unavailable to
break**. **Record: two breaches in three uses, and the third could not have gone the other way.** Carried
into `§5` R-001 **(a2)**, which until now read *decided* rather than *applied*.

**W-3 · Confirm CI's conclusion. — ✅ DONE 2026-10-27.**

superseded; this is a pass, not a supersede. CI only began triggering on task branches on 2026-10-26

verified from here:* that its body matches `docs/tickets/QRPAGER-9-pr.md` — reading a PR body needs `gh`
and a token, and this environment has neither. The merge itself is verified from the repo: `main` contains
the commit, and its tree is identical to the branch head `2e24135`.

**W-6 · (deferred) Branch protection on `main`.**
*What:* nothing to do now — the ruleset is configured and inert. *Why no agent:* a GitHub plan and
ownership decision. *How verified:* at handover, after **Transfer Ownership to an Organization**, a direct
push to `main` is rejected. R-001's review-by is that transfer or a second developer, whichever is first.

**W-7 · Tag `task-2` and push the tag. — ✅ DONE 2026-10-27, after one correction.**
*Outcome:* `task-2` resolves to **`2e42267`**, the squash merge of PR #3, locally and on the remote.
**It did not at first.** The tag was initially pushed on **`7f1c8f6`** — the docs commit immediately before
the merge, a tree with **none** of Task 2's code in it: of the 126 files between the two, **94 are
additions** and 15 149 lines are insertions, including `docs/openapi.json` and the entire
`apps/api/app/Http/Middleware` tree. It was **deleted on the remote and recreated** on `2e42267` rather
than force-moved, so the tag gets its **own** CI run in its own concurrency group — what `c07bae6` fixed,
and what gave `task-1`'s tag a verdict of its own.
*What caught it:* this entry's own *how verified* clause — and only half of it. "`task-2` resolves to the
commit on `main`" was **true of the wrong commit**, because `7f1c8f6` is also on `main`. The check that
discriminates is the **tree**, not the branch: `git diff task-2 2e42267` was 126 files, and is now empty.
Recorded because R-025 — a claim this file asserts that measurement contradicts — is this project's most
frequent defect, and this is an instance of it in the **delivery record** rather than in prose.
*The other half, now closed:* **W-3** — CI is green on `2e42267`, all three jobs, reported by the user on
2026-10-27. **Order matters and `§6` states it: no task starts before the previous one is green and
tagged** — and `2e42267` is now both, on one commit rather than two that look alike. Task 3 starts clear.

**R-025 is also assigned to Muhammad and is not an action** — it is the standing instruction that a claim
in this file is checked against measurement before it is written. It belongs in `§5`, not here, but it is
the reason every row above says how it will be verified rather than only what it is.


### The eighth review — the bar, set before it ran

> Written here verbatim, by the user, **before** the review was commissioned, so that what counts as
> blocking was not decided under fatigue after reading its findings.

> "Eighth review, final. Blocking: only a cross-tenant read or write reachable WITHOUT a raw set_config in a
> callback — that is, a failure of the invariant Task 1 exists to guarantee. Everything else, at any severity,
> goes to §5 with an owner and a date. Seven reviews have confirmed tenant isolation holds; the findings since
> the fifth round are durability, message content and record accuracy, each round of fixing which has produced
> more. The branch ships on the invariant, with the remainder documented."

Each ticket carries its own acceptance gate **copied verbatim from §3 below**, the §6 pre-coding questions as a checklist, an explicit _out of scope_ table naming which task owns each excluded item, and the known traps for that task. Tickets are linked `blocks` in order.

### How a task runs

1. Open the ticket. Read `PROJECT_INSTRUCTIONS.md`, this file, `docs/adr/`.
   **Including `§3.1` and `§3.2` for this task's own row** — what the gate still needs, and which
   `§5` rows the task owns with whether its gate could catch them. Task 2 began with `§0` naming 6
   debt items while `§5`'s owner column assigned 17, and nobody noticed until both were read.
2. Answer the seven §6 pre-coding questions **as a comment on the ticket**. Then move to In Progress.
3. Branch `task-N-<slug>`. Build. Three gates green against real Postgres + Redis.
4. PR. Both required checks green.
5. **QA Review** — separate agent session, diff + ticket only.
6. **Security Review** — separate agent session, diff + ticket only.
7. Every Critical and High fixed with a regression test. Medium / Low → §5 with owner and date.
8. Squash merge. Tag `task-N`. Push the tag.
9. **Update §0 of this file, and §4a with the gate evidence.**

## 2. Data contract — filled at Task 1

> Blueprint §3.1 is superseded (C3). **The Laravel migrations in `apps/api/database/migrations` are the
> source of truth; this section transcribes them.** Any task that needs a new table, column, enum value,
> role, GUC or grant updates this section **first** (`PROJECT_INSTRUCTIONS.md §6.1`) and then writes the
> migration. Nothing here may be invented by a later task.

### 2.1 Roles and connections

Four roles exist in the cluster, but only **three are application roles** — the fourth is the cluster's
pre-existing bootstrap superuser, which exists whether or not we name it.

| Role             | Attributes                                                                                         | Used by                                                        | Laravel connection        |
| ---------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------- |
| `qrpager_root`   | SUPERUSER (local) / `rds_superuser` (Aurora)                                                       | **Role and database bootstrap only**, never by the application | `pgsql_root`              |
| `qrpager_owner`  | NOSUPERUSER **NOBYPASSRLS** NOCREATEDB NOCREATEROLE, owns every table, `CREATE` on `schema public` | Migrations only                                                | `pgsql_owner`             |
| `qrpager_app`    | NOSUPERUSER NOBYPASSRLS NOCREATEDB NOCREATEROLE, **no `CREATE` on any schema**                     | Web requests                                                   | `pgsql` ← **the default** |
| `qrpager_worker` | same attributes as `qrpager_app`                                                                   | Queue worker                                                   | `pgsql_worker`            |

**Why `root` is separate from `owner`.** Verified in-session: a Postgres **superuser ignores `ENABLE` and
`FORCE` row level security entirely** — it read both tenants' rows with no tenant context set. Task 0's
container bootstrapped its superuser _as_ `qrpager_owner`, so the §3 gate clause "`owner` is also bound by
FORCE RLS" was unsatisfiable by construction. The bootstrap superuser is therefore renamed `qrpager_root`
and `qrpager_owner` is created as a **non-superuser** table owner. This maps to production exactly: the
Aurora master user bootstraps the roles once, migrations run as `owner`, and neither is the runtime role.

`DB_OWNER_*` and `DB_ROOT_*` credentials belong **only** to the migration/CI environment, and a runtime task
should carry `DB_USERNAME=qrpager_app` (or `qrpager_worker`) and nothing else.

**Corrected 2026-10-25 (F6, security review of task-1).** This paragraph used to claim that
`assertSafeRuntimeRole()` "fails the boot if it ever carries more". It does not, and never did. What exists
is narrower: the guard asks **one connection** for its `current_user` and refuses a role that can see past
row level security — owner, superuser, `BYPASSRLS`, or a member of the owner. It does **not** inspect the
environment, so a runtime task that also carries `DB_OWNER_PASSWORD` passes it; and `withoutTenantScope()`
is a global function defaulting to the owner connection, so request-handling code is one call from a
cross-tenant bypass that is audited but not prevented. Nothing calls the guard yet either. Both halves —
wiring it into boot, and widening it to refuse a resolvable non-runtime credential — are **Task 2's**, whose
gate carries "boot refuses a runtime task that can resolve owner or root credentials".

### 2.2 GUCs — the only two that exist (C1)

| GUC                 | Scope                                                                             | Set by                                                                        | Read by                            |
| ------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------- |
| `app.tenant_id`     | **transaction-local always** — `SELECT set_config('app.tenant_id', ?, true)` (C2) | `withTenant()`                                                                | every tenant policy                |
| `app.bypass_tenant` | transaction-local | **two setters, and only these two:** `withoutTenantScope()` — owner connection only, writes an `audit_log` row — **and** the body of each `§2.8` `SECURITY DEFINER` resolver, which turns it on for the duration of its own body and restores the previous value before returning. The second arrived with Task 3 (`resolve_pager_token`); this cell named only the first until 2026-10-27, although `§2.8` has prescribed the other since Task 1 | the `owner_bypass_*` policies only |

**Verified, and the reason the `NULLIF` guard is not optional:** a placeholder GUC that has _never_ been set
returns `NULL`, but once a transaction has set it, **every later transaction on that connection reads an
empty string `''`** — and `''::uuid` raises `22P02`, it does not match nothing. Every policy therefore uses
`NULLIF(current_setting('app.tenant_id', true), '')::uuid`, which yields `NULL`, which makes the policy
false, which is "**0 rows, no error**".

**`app.bypass_tenant` is not a general escape hatch.** Every `owner_bypass_*` policy is `TO qrpager_owner`.
Verified: `qrpager_app` setting `app.bypass_tenant = 'on'` itself still reads **0 rows**. SQL injection in
the web tier therefore cannot reach another tenant's data — the GUC is inert for the runtime roles.

### 2.3 Enum types — one source, mechanically checked

The **PHP enum is the source**. The Postgres type is a native `ENUM` created by migration, and
`tests/Feature/Database/EnumContractTest.php` compares `pg_enum` labels against the PHP cases for every
pair in `App\Domain\DatabaseEnums::map()`, so adding a case to either side fails the build until both
agree. Task 8 generates `packages/contracts` from the same registry (ADR-003) — never a hand-written copy.

| Postgres type      | PHP enum                          | Values (ordered)                                            |
| ------------------ | --------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------- |
| `pager_status`     | `App\Domain\Pager\PagerStatus`    | `waiting` · `ready` · `completed` · `cancelled` · `expired` |
| `outbox_status`    | `App\Domain\Outbox\OutboxStatus`  | `pending` · `sent` · `dead`                                 |
| `tenant_status`    | `App\Domain\Tenancy\TenantStatus` | `active` · `suspended`                                      |
| `tenant_role`      | `App\Domain\Tenancy\TenantRole`   | `owner` · `manager` · `staff`                               |
| `audit_actor_type` | `App\Domain\Audit\AuditActorType` | `user` · `system` · `platform`                              |

`tenant_role` holds **tenant-scoped** roles only. The platform-admin flag is Task 5's and is not in this
contract yet. Transitions of `pager_status` are defined in the PHP enum by Task 3, not here.

### 2.4 Global tables — no `tenant_id`, no RLS

A table with no tenant scope column must appear in this list **and** in the allowlist in
`tests/Feature/Database/RlsCoverageTest.php`, or the meta-test fails the build. That is what stops a new
table from quietly escaping tenant isolation.

| Table                   | Columns                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            | Grants                                     |
| ----------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------ |
| `migrations`            | framework                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          | `owner` only                               |
| `users`                 | `id` uuid PK · `name` varchar(120) · `email` varchar(255) **unique**, stored lower-cased · `email_verified_at` timestamptz NULL · `password` varchar(255) NULL _(passkey-only users have none)_ · `remember_token` varchar(100) NULL · `created_at` · `updated_at` | app: SELECT/INSERT/UPDATE · worker: SELECT |
| `password_reset_tokens` | `email` varchar(255) PK · `token` varchar(255) · `created_at`                                                                                                                                                                                                      | app: SELECT/INSERT/DELETE                  |
| `failed_jobs`           | Laravel standard (`id` bigint identity · `uuid` unique · `connection` · `queue` · `payload` · `exception` · `failed_at`)                                                                                                                                           | **worker only**: INSERT/SELECT/DELETE      |

`users` is global because a user may hold memberships in several tenants and platform staff belong to none.
`failed_jobs` is granted to the worker **only**: a serialized job payload can contain tenant data, and a
global table has no RLS behind it.

**Dropped from the Laravel skeleton:** `sessions`, `cache`, `cache_locks`, `jobs`, `job_batches`. Redis is
the locked store for session, cache and queue (`PROJECT_INSTRUCTIONS.md §1`), so these were five unused
tables, four of which would have needed a tenant-isolation justification they cannot have.

### 2.5 Tenant tables — `ENABLE` + `FORCE` RLS on every one (C4)

All timestamps are `timestamptz`. All ids are `uuid` except `audit_log.id`.
"Scope column" is the column every policy compares against `app.tenant_id`.

| Table                | Scope column             | Columns                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
| -------------------- | ------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `tenants`            | **`id`**                 | `id` PK · `name` varchar(120) · `slug` varchar(63) **unique**, CHECK DNS-label · `status` `tenant_status` default `active` · `timezone` varchar(64) default `UTC` · `created_at` · `updated_at`                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
| `tenant_users`       | `tenant_id`              | `id` PK · `tenant_id` → `tenants.id` RESTRICT · `user_id` → `users.id` CASCADE · `role` `tenant_role` · `created_at` · `updated_at` · UNIQUE `(tenant_id, user_id)` · INDEX `(user_id)`                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
| `pager_sessions`     | `tenant_id`              | `id` PK · `tenant_id` → `tenants.id` RESTRICT · `token_hash` bytea **unique** · `order_reference` varchar(32) · `status` `pager_status` · `expires_at` · `ready_at` NULL · `completed_at` NULL · `cancelled_at` NULL · `expired_at` NULL · `last_notified_at` NULL · `renotify_count` smallint default 0 CHECK ≥ 0 · `created_by_user_id` → `users.id` SET NULL · `created_at` · `updated_at` · UNIQUE `(tenant_id, id)` · INDEX `(tenant_id, status, created_at DESC)` · partial INDEX `(expires_at)` WHERE `status IN ('waiting','ready')`                        |
| `push_subscriptions` | `tenant_id`              | `id` PK · `tenant_id` · `pager_session_id` · **FK `(tenant_id, pager_session_id)` → `pager_sessions (tenant_id, id)` CASCADE** · `endpoint` text CHECK `https://` + ≤ 2048 · `p256dh` varchar(255) · `auth` varchar(255) · `created_at` · `updated_at` · UNIQUE `(pager_session_id, endpoint)` · INDEX `(tenant_id)`                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |
| `outbox`             | `tenant_id`              | `id` PK · `tenant_id` → `tenants.id` RESTRICT · `pager_session_id` NULL · **FK `(tenant_id, pager_session_id)` → `pager_sessions (tenant_id, id)` CASCADE** · `type` varchar(64) · `dedupe_key` varchar(160) · `payload` jsonb default `{}` · `status` `outbox_status` default `pending` · `attempts` smallint default 0 CHECK ≥ 0 · `available_at` default `now()` · `sent_at` NULL · `dead_at` NULL · `last_error` text NULL · `created_at` · `updated_at` · **UNIQUE `(tenant_id, dedupe_key)`** · partial INDEX `(available_at, id)` WHERE `status = 'pending'` |
| `audit_log`          | `tenant_id` **nullable** | `id` bigint `GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY` PK · `tenant_id` NULL = platform scope, **no FK** · `actor_type` `audit_actor_type` · `actor_user_id` uuid NULL, **no FK** · `action` varchar(64) · `subject_type` varchar(64) NULL · `subject_id` uuid NULL · `context` jsonb default `{}` · `ip_address` inet NULL · `created_at` default `now()` · INDEX `(tenant_id, created_at DESC)` · INDEX `(action, created_at DESC)`                                                                                                                                           |

Three column choices that are load-bearing, not incidental:

- **`UNIQUE (tenant_id, id)` on `pager_sessions`** exists so `push_subscriptions` and `outbox` can carry a
  **composite** foreign key. That is what makes "a push subscription cannot point at another tenant's
  session" structurally impossible rather than policy-dependent: referential integrity checks bypass RLS,
  so the composite key is checked against the real table and a cross-tenant reference raises `23503`.
  Verified in-session.
- **`UNIQUE (tenant_id, dedupe_key)` on `outbox`** is the mechanism for Task 3's idempotent `markReady`:
  one row per logical event, enforced by the database under true concurrency, not by a read-then-write check.
- **`token_hash` is `bytea`, and PDO will not bind raw binary to it.** Bind hex and decode in SQL:
  `insert into pager_sessions (…, token_hash, …) values (…, decode(?, 'hex'), …)`, and the same for the
  lookup's `where token_hash = decode(?, 'hex')`. Task 3 inherits this; `tests/TestCase.php` has the
  working shape.
- **`audit_log` carries no foreign keys.** An `ON DELETE SET NULL` would be an `UPDATE`, which the
  append-only trigger blocks, so deleting a user would become impossible. The log keeps ids, not references.

### 2.6 Policy matrix

Every tenant table gets exactly these, where `ctx` is `NULLIF(current_setting('app.tenant_id', true), '')::uuid`:

| Policy                     | Command | Roles              | `USING`         | `WITH CHECK`    |
| -------------------------- | ------- | ------------------ | --------------- | --------------- |
| `tenant_isolation_<table>` | ALL     | app, worker, owner | `scope = ctx`   | `scope = ctx`   |
| `owner_bypass_<table>`     | ALL     | **owner only**     | `bypass = 'on'` | `bypass = 'on'` |

Plus exactly these named cross-tenant policies, and no others:

| Policy                          | Table            | Command | Role   | `USING`               | `WITH CHECK`             | §4 named path    |
| ------------------------------- | ---------------- | ------- | ------ | --------------------- | ------------------------ | ---------------- |
| `worker_drains_outbox`          | `outbox`         | SELECT  | worker | `true`                | —                        | the outbox queue |
| `worker_updates_outbox`         | `outbox`         | UPDATE  | worker | `true`                | `true`                   | the outbox queue |
| `worker_reads_expired_sessions` | `pager_sessions` | SELECT  | worker | `expires_at <= now() AND status IN ('waiting','ready','expired')` | —                        | the expiry job   |
| `worker_expires_sessions`       | `pager_sessions` | UPDATE  | worker | `expires_at <= now() AND status IN ('waiting','ready')` | **`status = 'expired'`** | the expiry job   |

**The two `pager_sessions` policies were narrowed at Task 3 (2026-10-27), which is what closed `R-003`.**
They were bounded by time alone, and `expires_at` only recedes into the past, so the worker held a
permanent context-free cross-tenant **read** of every historical session and a cross-tenant **write** over
every terminal row — it could mark another tenant's `completed` order `expired`, with no audit row. Both now
carry a status whitelist. Measured on 16.14 as part of that change: another tenant's `completed` or
`cancelled` row is no longer selectable at all, and the expiry update against it reports `UPDATE 0`, while
the prescribed `SELECT id → UPDATE … WHERE id = ? AND status = ?` still reports `UPDATE 1` on a `waiting`
or `ready` row past its TTL and any other transition on a visible row still raises `42501`.

**`'expired'` is in the SELECT whitelist and not only in the UPDATE one, and that is `§2.12` arriving as
documented rather than a widening.** An `UPDATE` that references a column re-checks the **new** row against
the SELECT policies, so the whitelist has to contain the status the update writes. Measured: with
`'expired'` removed from `worker_reads_expired_sessions`, the prescribed statement raises `42501` and every
expiry would fail. `PROJECT_INSTRUCTIONS.md §4 › Postgres traps` prescribes exactly this shape — "a status
whitelist containing the status the update writes" — and this is the counterfactual behind it.

Permissive policies are OR-ed, so the worker's cross-tenant reach is _exactly_ these four grants and is
further narrowed by column grants: it can never `INSERT` into `outbox`, never change `tenant_id` anywhere,
and cross-tenant it can move a session to **no status other than `expired`**, only from `waiting` or
`ready`, and only after its TTL has passed. `app` has no cross-tenant policy on any table.

The remaining two named paths in `§4 › Tenancy` — **the QR token lookup** and **a user's own memberships** —
have no policy here on purpose. They are implemented by the task that needs them (3 and 5) with the recipe
in §2.8, because a policy wide enough to serve them would be a standing bypass for `qrpager_app`.

### 2.7 Column grants — what each role may touch

The point of column grants is that over-broad SQL cannot leak what the role was never given.

| Table                | `qrpager_app`                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      | `qrpager_worker`                                                                             |
| -------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `tenants`            | SELECT all · INSERT `(id, name, slug, status, timezone, created_at, updated_at)` · UPDATE `(name, status, timezone, updated_at)`                                                                                                                                   | SELECT all                                                                                   |
| `tenant_users`       | SELECT · INSERT · UPDATE `(role, updated_at)` · DELETE                                                                                                                                                                                                             | SELECT                                                                                       |
| `pager_sessions`     | SELECT **all except `token_hash`** · INSERT `(id, tenant_id, token_hash, order_reference, status, expires_at, created_by_user_id, created_at, updated_at)` · UPDATE `(status, ready_at, completed_at, cancelled_at, last_notified_at, renotify_count, updated_at)` | SELECT **all except `token_hash`** · UPDATE `(status, expired_at, updated_at)`               |
| `push_subscriptions` | SELECT · INSERT · UPDATE `(p256dh, auth, updated_at)` · DELETE                                                                                                                                                                                                     | SELECT · DELETE _(404/410 pruning)_                                                          |
| `outbox`             | SELECT · INSERT `(id, tenant_id, pager_session_id, type, dedupe_key, payload, available_at, created_at, updated_at)`                                                                                                                                               | SELECT · UPDATE `(status, attempts, available_at, sent_at, dead_at, last_error, updated_at)` |
| `audit_log`          | INSERT `(tenant_id, actor_type, actor_user_id, action, subject_type, subject_id, context, ip_address)` · SELECT                                                                                                                                                    | INSERT same · SELECT                                                                         |

- **`token_hash` is write-only for `app`.** Verified: a role can `INSERT` a column it cannot `SELECT`, so the
  hash can be stored and never read back — the raw token is already forbidden anywhere (`§4 › Data`), and
  the hash cannot leak through a careless `SELECT *` either. Task 3's lookup goes through §2.8.
- **No role has `UPDATE` on any `tenant_id`**, so a row cannot be moved between tenants even with context set.
- **No role has `UPDATE`, `DELETE` or `TRUNCATE` on `audit_log`**; the trigger in §2.9 is the backstop behind that.
- `app` has no `DELETE` on `pager_sessions` or `outbox`: sessions are cancelled or expire, they are not deleted.

### 2.8 Cross-tenant reads — the only mechanism

A named path in `§4 › Tenancy` that genuinely has no tenant context is implemented as a
**`SECURITY DEFINER` function owned by `qrpager_owner`** with `SET search_path`, which turns on
`app.bypass_tenant` for the duration of its own body, restores the previous value before returning, and is
granted `EXECUTE` to `qrpager_app` alone. Verified end to end, including that the GUC does **not** leak back
to the caller and that the caller's own direct queries stay tenant-bound.

**Two rules, both learned by running it:**

1. **Never `RETURNS SETOF <table>`.** A function returns rows as its owner, and **column grants are not
   applied to a function's result set** — a `SETOF pager_sessions` resolver hands `token_hash` straight back
   to `qrpager_app` and quietly undoes §2.7. Declare a narrow `RETURNS TABLE (...)` naming only the columns
   the caller needs.
2. The function body is the audit boundary. It takes one exact-match argument (a token hash, a user id), so
   it answers one question and cannot be used to enumerate.

### 2.9 `audit_log` immutability

A `BEFORE UPDATE OR DELETE` statement-level trigger and a `BEFORE TRUNCATE` statement-level trigger both
raise `42501`, and both are **`ENABLE ALWAYS`**.

`ENABLE ALWAYS` is the whole point, and it is not decoration: verified in-session, a **superuser** that sets
`session_replication_role = replica` deletes rows straight through an ordinary trigger, and is refused by an
`ENABLE ALWAYS` one. Statement-level is deliberate — it fires even when the statement would match zero rows.

Immutability holds against every role including the superuser. It does **not** hold against a superuser
willing to run DDL (`DROP TRIGGER`), which nothing in Postgres can prevent; in production no application
role is a superuser and `owner` holds no `SUPERUSER` or `BYPASSRLS` attribute.

### 2.10 Data migrations under FORCE RLS — the settled pattern

`PLAN.md §6` recorded this as the trap that blocks the first data migration anyone writes. `owner` is bound
by FORCE RLS, so a migration that touches rows sees **none** of them — including the `tenants` table it wants
to loop over. **Corrected 2026-10-25 (H-1, QA review of task-1):** pattern 1 below used to open with a plain
`DB::connection('pgsql_owner')->table('tenants')->pluck('id')`. That returns **zero rows** inside a migration,
so the loop body never ran. A backfill that silently does nothing is worse than one that fails loudly.

Two patterns, in order of preference:

```php
// 1. Preferred — the per-tenant work needs no bypass. Enumerating the tenants does:
//    that one cross-tenant read is the only part the owner cannot do under FORCE RLS.
$ids = withoutTenantScope(
    '2026_11_02 backfill pager_sessions.order_reference: list tenants',
    fn () => DB::connection('pgsql_owner')->table('tenants')->pluck('id'),
);

foreach ($ids as $id) {
    // Keyed, never bulk: an UPDATE or DELETE that references no column of the table
    // escapes RLS row filtering entirely (PROJECT_INSTRUCTIONS.md §4 › Postgres traps).
    withTenant($id, fn () => /* ... */, 'pgsql_owner');
}

// 2. Only when the work itself genuinely spans tenants — named, audited, owner-only:
withoutTenantScope('2026_11_02 backfill pager_sessions.order_reference', fn () => /* ... */);
```

Both helpers always run **nested** here: `Migrator` wraps every migration in a transaction because
`PostgresGrammar` reports `supportsSchemaTransactions`, so a migration body is already at transaction level 1.
`set_config(name, value, true)` binds to the **outermost** transaction and not to the savepoint Laravel opens
for the inner block, which is what made F1 a leak. **Corrected 2026-10-26 (L-5, fifth security review):** this
paragraph described a save-and-restore that the fifth round deleted, and had done since. There is no capture
and no restore of `app.tenant_id` any more. `withTenant()` writes `''` on its success path and _proves in the
same round trip that the scope it is clearing was still its own_ (M-2); `withoutTenantScope()` never touches
`app.tenant_id` at all and restores only the bypass GUC. The ordering hazard the old text was about is
unreachable rather than ordered against, because each helper refuses to open inside the other. Proven by
`DataMigrationPatternTest`, and by the composition matrix for the refusals.

`withoutTenantScope()` refuses to run on any connection whose role is not `qrpager_owner`, and writes an
`audit_log` row (`actor_type = system`, `action = tenancy.bypass`, the reason in `context`) inside the same
transaction, so a bypass that **commits** always carries its own audit row. A bypass that **throws** rolls
that row back with the work and leaves no trace: carried as F12 in `§5`, owned by Task 2. A **schema**
migration needs neither pattern; only a migration that reads or writes rows does.

A third pattern needs no bypass and is the one to reach for first: seed rows **before** the table's RLS is
enabled, within the same migration.

### 2.11 What is bounded, what is not

| Growth               | Bound                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   |
| -------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `pager_sessions`     | Unbounded by design (one row per order, forever). Rows reach a terminal status within 2 h of creation; the hot working set is the partial index on live sessions only. Retention is a Task 13/14 decision and is **not** solved here.                   |
| `push_subscriptions` | Bounded by live sessions — `ON DELETE CASCADE` from the session, plus 404/410 pruning in Task 4.                                                                                                                                                        |
| `outbox`             | **Unbounded, and the one to watch.** `sent` and `dead` rows are never deleted by any grant in §2.7. The worker's claim index is partial on `status = 'pending'`, so throughput does not degrade, but disk does. Pruning is unassigned — recorded in §6. |
| `audit_log`          | Unbounded by design and append-only, so pruning can only ever be a partition drop, never a `DELETE`. Recorded in §6.                                                                                                                                    |
| Per active session   | 1 `pager_sessions` row + 0-N `push_subscriptions` rows. No per-session Postgres read on the hot path: Task 4's polling endpoint is Redis-only (`§4 › Realtime`).                                                                                        |
| Per tenant scope     | **3 statements of overhead + 2 transaction round trips, accepted deliberately (D11).** Measured at Task 2 from the Postgres log rather than from PHP: one `withTenant()` scope containing one caller query is **4 statements as `DB::listen` reports them** — `readState`, `set_config`, the caller's query, the `MATERIALIZED` read-back-and-clear — and **6 round trips on the wire**, because `BEGIN` and `COMMIT` go through PDO and `DB::listen` never sees them. The overhead is independent of the work inside: a scope with three caller queries reports 6 statements, still 3 of overhead. The log also shows `readState` running **before** `BEGIN`, which is R-022 visible in a server log. Optimising this before any load number exists is what `§7` forbids; **Task 14 re-measures**. |
| Per request          | **Corrected 2026-10-27 (security #8).** This cell read "the two probes cost **0 Postgres and 0 Redis round trips** on the request path", and the Redis half was false: `ReadinessStore::read()` issues one `GET` on `qrpager:readiness`, on the request path, every time `/readyz` is called. `routes/probes.php` ("/readyz reads one Redis key") and `ShedLoad`'s docblock ("one Redis read") were both already right; this cell was the one place that was not — and it is the sentence that would have justified leaving the probe group unlimited, which is R-031. Measured: **`/healthz` 0 Postgres, 0 Redis** — it reads nothing at all. **`/readyz` 0 Postgres, 1 Redis** — the Postgres half is B6 and is asserted with a query listener over a real request (`ReadinessCarrierTest`); nothing asserted the Redis half, correctly, because it is one. A `/v1` request costs **1 Redis read for load shedding, 1-2 Redis round trips for the rate limiter** (per IP, plus per tenant when one resolves) and then its own units of work at the scope cost above. |

### 2.12 A verified Postgres behaviour that changes a documented trap

`PROJECT_INSTRUCTIONS.md §4 › Postgres traps` and the Task 3 ticket both explain the no-`RETURNING` rule as
"`RETURNING` makes Postgres apply SELECT policies to the NEW row, and expired rows are deliberately not
selectable by the worker". Run against Postgres 16.14, the first half is **narrower than reality** and the
second half would **break the expiry job outright**:

| Statement                                                                          | Result                                           |
| ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------ |
| `UPDATE … SET status='expired' WHERE id = ?` — new row hidden by the SELECT policy | **`ERROR 42501` — no `RETURNING` involved**      |
| same, with `RETURNING id`                                                          | `ERROR 42501`                                    |
| `UPDATE … SET status='expired'` with **no `WHERE`**                                | succeeds                                         |
| `UPDATE … SET status='ready'` — new row still visible                              | succeeds                                         |
| `UPDATE … WHERE id = ?` — **old** row hidden by the SELECT policy                  | `UPDATE 0` — silent, no error                    |
| `UPDATE … SET status='touched'` with **no `WHERE`** — old row hidden               | succeeds — **writes a row the role cannot read** |

The actual rule: **when an `UPDATE` reads any column of the table, the SELECT policies are applied to the
new row as well.** The prescribed pattern `SELECT id → UPDATE … WHERE id = ? AND status = ?` reads columns,
so a worker SELECT policy that hid expired rows would make the expiry job impossible — `42501` every time,
or a silent `UPDATE 0` if no cross-tenant SELECT policy existed at all.

The same trigger has an **old-row half**, and it is the more dangerous one: when a column is referenced a row
invisible to the SELECT policy is silently skipped (`UPDATE 0`, no error), but when **no** column is referenced
the filter does not apply at all and the statement writes rows the role cannot read — measured as `UPDATE 2`
where the same statement with a `WHERE` reported `UPDATE 0`.

This is why `worker_reads_expired_sessions` is bounded by **time** (`expires_at <= now()`) and not by status:
the new row still satisfies it. The no-`RETURNING` rule is kept — it costs nothing and still protects any
future policy that hides a new row — but it is now a defensive convention, not the mechanism. **The §4
wording was corrected on 2026-10-25 with the user's approval (rank 2, `§0`): the trigger is restated as any
column reference by the statement, the old-row asymmetry is recorded as its own trap, and the claim that
expired rows are not selectable by the worker is dropped.** The time-only bound on
`worker_reads_expired_sessions` is wider than the path it implements and is carried as an accepted risk
(`§5` R-003, owner Task 3, which also owns the expiry job).

---

| **1**  | **DB foundation.** Migrations for the full schema, 3 DB roles, RLS `ENABLE` + `FORCE` on every tenant table, per-role policies, column grants, append-only `audit_log` trigger, `ALTER ROLE` timeouts, `withTenant()` transaction helper, `assertSafeRuntimeRole()`.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   | No tenant context → every table returns **0 rows, no error**; cross-tenant read/update/delete/insert all blocked; `owner` is also bound by FORCE RLS; context does **not** leak across a single-connection pool; `worker` sees the outbox across tenants, `app` does not; `audit_log` immutable even for superuser; **meta-test fails the build if any table lacks ENABLE or FORCE**; **a push subscription cannot point at another tenant's session**; **the `ALTER ROLE` timeouts are verified to fire, per role**; migrations apply clean from empty twice; **`migrate:rollback` runs clean and a re-apply returns the identical schema** (Blueprint §7 Database). §2 of this file is filled.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        |
| **2**  | **API core.** Octane/FrankenPHP boot, config validation, tenant-context middleware (`set_config(..., true)` per transaction), safe-role boot refusal, error mapping to real driver codes, Monolog redaction, rate limiting, health + readiness, graceful shutdown, **named architecture guards in CI** (domain logic imports no framework or DB code · platform layer imports no feature modules · production code imports no test helpers), **a repo guard that rejects session-scoped tenant settings and unknown `app.*` names**, **OpenAPI spec generation wired**, **`/v1` route versioning**.                    | Boot refuses an unsafe DB role (exit 1, reason logged); a request without tenant context never reaches the DB; health reflects real Postgres + Redis state; `SIGTERM` drains in-flight requests and exits 0 (proven against the compiled app); captured logs contain no SQL, no bound parameters, no secrets; **the repo guard and each named architecture guard fail on a deliberately bad file** (C1/C2 enforced mechanically); **every public route is under `/v1`, and the versioning policy for a breaking change is written into §6**; **the rate limiter covers unknown routes (404s), or the gap is written into §5 with the control that covers it**; **a saturated DB pool returns a fast 503, and memory/CPU pressure returns 503 — while health and readiness keep answering**; **Redis is taken down and the API keeps serving, with the rate limiter's fail-open/fail-closed posture stated and tested**; **the OpenAPI spec is generated from the routes, committed, and CI fails when it is stale** (Blueprint §7 API); **a connection returned to the pool carries no open transaction and no tenant setting, proven by two consecutive operations on one connection**; **no error response body carries a database role name, a constraint name, SQL or a bound parameter, proven against captured response bodies**; **no test mutates the attributes of a permanent database role, proven by killing the suite mid-test and reading `pg_roles`**. (The last three were added on 2026-10-26 with the user's approval — rank 3, Q2 of `QRPAGER-9`. §3.2 found that ten of the seventeen §5 rows this task owns were named by nothing in the gate, so a reviewer handed the gate alone had no reason to look for them: clause (a) covers R-022 and R-020, (b) covers R-021 and R-007, (c) covers R-018. Clause (a) was measured as FALSE when it was written — the next request on an Octane worker entered at transaction level 1 carrying the previous request's tenant uuid — so it is a clause with a red test behind it rather than an aspiration.) |

### 3.1 Gate audit — what each task ships on, and what each gate still needs

Audited 2026-10-26, when `PROJECT_INSTRUCTIONS.md §11` gained its **Round limit**: after the second review
of a fix diff, only a failure of the task's **own stated invariant** blocks the tag. That makes the §3 gate
the thing the task ships on, so a gate naming nothing falsifiable leaves the cap with no bar to fall back on.

**The gates are deliberately NOT reworded here.** Each is fixed at the start of its own task, from this list,
by the person who is about to do that work — not in one pass today by someone who is not. What is recorded
here is every flag the audit raised, against the task that owns it, because _a finding nobody wrote down is a
finding nobody dispositions_ — which is `F5` of the seventh review, the one that cost a day.

Two exceptions went in immediately, both marked in the table above: **Task 3**, because the clause already
existed in the wrong column and moving it is free; and **Task 11**, because the audit found it has no
invariant at all and it is now marked **not ready to begin**.

| #      | What the task ships on — the invariant a review can falsify                                                                                                                                                                                      | What this gate still needs, at the start of that task                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           |
| ------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **2**  | A request without tenant context never reaches the DB · logs carry no SQL, bound parameters or secrets · each named guard fails on a deliberately bad file                                                                                       | Nothing. Strong, and mechanically testable                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| **3**  | The status change, outbox row and audit row commit together or not at all · `markReady` is idempotent under concurrent calls · cross-tenant IDOR blocked through HTTP, not just at the DB · the raw token never reaches a log, URL or error body | **Done 2026-10-26** — the one-transaction clause moved into the gate                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            |
| **4**  | Kill the worker mid-batch → nothing lost, nothing duplicated · every Redis key holding tenant data is tenant-namespaced and one tenant's projection cannot be read through another's context                                                     | `p99 < 5 ms` on the polling endpoint needs a stated measurement method. It is `F10`'s class: on this branch a loaded host turned a passing timeout proof into a red at **27.6 s**, and only the _lower_ bound survived as sound. Under the round limit a misleading red costs a round                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           |
| **5**  | A tenant-provisioning actor cannot read another tenant's data · device token scope cannot escalate · the pre-Task-5 auth stub is gone, by three assertions (added 2026-10-27, R-035)                                                                                                                                               | "Full role-matrix test" does not say what full means. Name the matrix — roles × actions × own-tenant/other-tenant — or it is unfalsifiable by anyone but its author                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
| **6**  | The wizard is transactional — a failure halfway leaves no partial tenant · platform admin actions all land in `audit_log`                                                                                                                        | Nothing on falsifiability. Note the separate blocker already in §6: platform-admin cross-tenant reads are **not** on `§4`'s named list, and Task 6 needs that settled before it builds the dashboard                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            |
| **7**  | An unpublished draft is not reachable · no script injection through any block · one tenant's asset path cannot be guessed or reached from another                                                                                                | "Theme overrides **tokens only, never components**" names no mechanism. Same empty-phrase shape the fourth review found in "architecture guards" — it needs a lint rule proven to fail on a bad file, or it is not checkable                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    |
| **8**  | The TS status mirror is generated and CI fails when it is stale · zero hard-coded hex/spacing/font-size, lint rule proven to fail on a bad file                                                                                                  | Two clauses a review **cannot** falsify: "a direction is approved in writing" is a process artifact, and "a reviewer cannot name the UI framework from a screenshot" depends on which reviewer looks. Keep them as goals; they cannot be what the task ships on                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| **9**  | A rotated subscription is re-registered by the `pushsubscriptionchange` handler and the next notification still arrives · the four failure paths are handled                                                                                     | `Lighthouse ≥ 95` needs a stated measurement method (`F10`'s class). "One-handed operation verified at 390px" needs a criterion — verified against what?                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        |
| **10** | KDS survives a network drop and resyncs **without duplicate notifications** · the board stays correct with two staff acting at once                                                                                                              | "Under 2 s to the customer device" is a wall-clock threshold with no stated method (`F10`'s class). "The board stays correct" needs _correct_ defined — which rows, in which order, after which interleaving                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    |
| **11** | **None. ⛔ Not ready to begin**                                                                                                                                                                                                                  | **The missing invariant: tenant A's page can never render tenant B's content** — per-tenant theme, content, assets and domain, proven by requesting tenant A's domain and asserting nothing of tenant B's appears in the HTML, the cached response or the asset URLs. Everything the current gate names (cold CDN serving, theme applied, "SEO/OG tags correct", Lighthouse ≥ 95) is either a deliverable or an unstated threshold. This is the only surface where a cross-tenant leak is **published publicly and CDN-cached**, so it outlives its own fix                     |
| **12** | CSP has no `unsafe-inline` · the fuzz suite finds no 5xx · the OpenAPI spec exposes no endpoint that should be private                                                                                                                           | "Zero high/critical from SAST and DAST" is a tool-output claim: a clean run is not proof, only an absence of findings. Keep it, and do not let it stand in for the three clauses above it                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       |
| **13** | No stored AWS keys anywhere                                                                                                                                                                                                                      | **Weak: one mechanical clause.** Everything else is an existence check — dashboards exist, PITR enabled, auto-scaling configured — which a review can confirm but which cannot interestingly fail. Add a falsifiable property: no secret material anywhere outside Secrets Manager, proven by scanning the plan **and** the repo                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |
| **14** | **None falsifiable**                                                                                                                                                                                                                             | Every clause is a deliverable or a process step: results file written, launch tier set, drill executed, RTO written down, runbooks reviewed. Two fixes. (1) The chaos scenarios in the task column — task kill, Redis failover, DB failover — have **no stated property**; Task 4's "nothing lost, nothing duplicated" is the template and should be applied to each. (2) The restore drill should state the property rather than the artifact: a PITR restore returns a database that **passes Task 1's isolation suite** and loses no committed data beyond the stated window |

**The pattern across the weak gates.** The further a task is from the database, the more its gate drifts from
a property into a checklist — Tasks 13 and 14 are almost entirely existence checks, and Task 11 has no
property at all. That is the opposite of where the risk sits for Task 11 specifically, whose output is public
and cached.

### 3.2 Ledger sweep — every §5 row against its owner's own gate

Swept 2026-10-26, after §0's Task 2 debt list (6 items) was found to disagree with §5's owner
column (17 rows). §3.1 audits **gates** for falsifiability; this audits the **debt ledger** against
those gates. One mechanical question per row: _does the owning task's §3 gate name anything that
would catch this row if it were simply not fixed?_ A row answered **No** is owned by a task whose
reviewer, handed the gate alone, has no reason to look for it — and under `§11 › Round limit` that
means it does not block `task-N`. Its "review by: at Task N's acceptance gate" is then a date with
no mechanism behind it.

**Nothing here is reworded, reassigned, closed or re-dispositioned.** One pass, record only.

**Count: 26 owner→task assignments. 2 caught, 5 partial, 19 not caught.**

**Structural findings.**

1. **Nineteen of twenty-six assignments are invisible to the gate that is supposed to enforce them.**
   The ledger's "review by: at Task N's acceptance gate" column is, for those nineteen, a date with no
   mechanism. This is not a Task 2 problem; Task 2 is only where it was first counted.
2. **Two wording gaps account for four rows.** No gate anywhere names **error response bodies**
   (R-007 Task 2 half, R-021) — every redaction clause in the project is scoped to _logs_. And
   "boot refuses an unsafe DB **role**" reads as covering the process's **credentials** and does not
   (R-015, R-020).
3. **§0's debt list exists for Task 2 only.** For every other task, §5's owner column is the sole
   record, and nothing instructs anyone to read it at task start. §3.1 is read at task start; §3.2
   must be too.
4. **`§6 Open items` is a second, unowned ledger** — no owner column, no date, no severity, no gate
   clause — and it assigns work to tasks in prose: platform-admin cross-tenant reads (Task 6, needing
   a rank-2 §4 edit), platform-scope `audit_log` rows (Task 6), `outbox`/`audit_log` retention
   (Task 13/14), queue transport (Task 13/14). **Task 6 therefore begins with two blockers that appear
   in no ledger this sweep covers.** Tasks 6 and 14 showing zero §5 rows above is an artefact of that,
   not an absence of debt.
5. R-004 and R-012 are each split across two tasks, and R-007 across two, with the halves given
   different dates. A split row is dispositioned when the _last_ half is, and nothing tracks that.

**Delta, later the same day (2026-10-26). The table above is left exactly as swept; this is what has
changed since, because a sweep that is not re-run when a row is added is stale the moment one is.**

| Task | §5 row | In §0 debt list | Owning task's §3 gate catches it |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **12** | **R-026** `dedoc/scramble` pinned pre-1.0 | n/a | **Partial, and differently** — Task 12's gate does name the spec ("the OpenAPI spec is checked against the live routes"), so a reviewer is at least looking at it; nothing in that clause would notice the generator's version or a behaviour change. What actually catches this row is **mechanical and continuous rather than gate-bound**: `composer openapi:check` diffs a fresh export on every CI run, so a change arrives as a spec diff on a deliberate upgrade. First row in this ledger whose control is a build step rather than a gate clause |
| **12** | **R-029** a proof's check could not tell a short read from a missing extension | n/a | **No** — Task 12's gate names SAST, DAST, fuzzing and an ASVS table; none of them reads a shell script for a pipeline whose producer-side failure is indistinguishable from the condition under test. What caught this one was **CI itself**, on the first run this branch ever had — one commit after the trigger was fixed (`547911d`), so that change paid for itself immediately. Swept the day it was written |
| **12** | **R-028** `.githooks/` unwired, so both hooks are inert in a fresh clone | n/a | **No** — Task 12's gate names SAST, DAST, fuzzing and an ASVS table, and none of them inspects whether a git hook is wired up in the clone the developer is using. The row's own control is a **documented setup step**, which is not a control at all: that is the shape `§4` had when it asserted a repo guard that did not exist (R-010). Swept here on the day it was written, which is the standing rule below |
| **12** (with 13) | **R-027** an attacker who downs Redis removes rate limiting | n/a | **No** — Task 12's gate names per-tenant and per-IP limits, a fuzz suite and SAST/DAST, and none of those takes Redis away. Its compensating control is **Task 13's** WAF rate rules, so the row's mechanism sits in a different task's gate from its owner's — a sixth instance of finding 1, created by this task rather than inherited |

**Task 2's seventeen rows are all dispositioned as of `88759c6`.** Each row's own "Review by" cell in
`§5` now names the commit that closed it and how, so the seventeen Task 2 lines above are **history, not
live debt** — they record what the gate could and could not catch at the time, which is the only reason
to keep them unedited. Finding 2's two wording gaps were closed the same day by the three rank-3 gate
clauses (Q2) now in `§3` row 2: error response bodies, connection state at the pool boundary, and role
attributes mutated by tests.

**Revised count: 30 owner→task assignments. 2 caught, 6 partial, 22 not caught.** The ratio did not
improve — nineteen became twenty — and both new rows were written by the task that had just finished
counting the problem, which is the honest measure of how easy it is to add a row whose owner's gate
cannot see it.

finding 1.

thing evidenced. This delta adds two more — `R-054` about what the gate log holds and `R-057` about what it
holds that it should not — so the category is no longer a one-off: **three of fifty-eight assignments are
about the toolchain, and no `§3` gate has a clause any of them could fail.** `§6`'s "fresh clone, not just a
fresh DB" remains the nearest thing and is still about the environment a gate runs in. The honest reading is
that `§3.1`'s question — what invariant can a review falsify? — is asked only of the product, and a third
of this round's rows were not reachable by it.

**Standing rule, from the third delta:** a new `§5` row is swept into this subsection **when it is
written**, with its three answers, not at the next task's start. Writing a row and assessing it are the
same act; splitting them is what produced nineteen unassessed assignments.

| `gitleaks detect` clean                                         | clean locally and in CI over full history (`fetch-depth: 0`)                                                                                                 |

Built: Laravel 13.38.0 in `apps/api`, Docker Compose (Postgres 16.14 + Redis 7, bound to `127.0.0.1` only, `log_statement=all` for the Task 4 proof), Pint + PHPStan level 8 (larastan) + Pest 5, gitleaks pre-commit + conventional-commit hook (both proven to block), `CLAUDE.md`, `.claude/agents/{qa,security}-reviewer.md`, `.claude/commands/task.md`, GitHub Actions.

**Bugs found and fixed in Task 0 — do not repeat:**

1. `.gitignore` had `/build/`, which anchors to the repo root, so `apps/api/build/` (the PHPStan `tmpDir`) was committed — 678 cache files. Fixed to `build/`. Any new tool cache must be verified with `git status` after running the tool, not assumed ignored.
2. Deleting the last file in `tests/Unit/` left an empty directory that git does not track, so `phpunit.xml`'s `Unit` testsuite broke on a fresh CI clone while passing locally. Fixed with a real unit test asserting PHP 8.4 and the required extensions — never a `.gitkeep`.
3. `apps/api/CLAUDE.md` shipped by the Laravel skeleton (laravel-boost) instructed agents to install PHP 8.5, contradicting §1. Deleted; one `CLAUDE.md` at the repo root only.

**0 errors** · `php artisan test` **198 passed, 580 assertions** against the composed Postgres 16.14 and
Redis 7, from a **dropped and rebuilt** test database and again from a **fresh `git clone` of the branch**
with nothing but `cp .env.example .env`, `key:generate` and the running compose cluster · `gitleaks protect
--staged` passed on every commit locally (8.30.1, the CI pin) and `gitleaks detect` passed over full history

only at `263` and `275`, two pre-existing unreachable guard throws this task did not add. **No coverage
